I... I suppose it's technically possible that someone might not notice it. But it's really hard for me to believe that.

So even if someone else claims they don't see it, I just can't shake the feeling that the students, the parents, the other staff... they must all be staring right at it. I really feel like they aren't even listening to anything I'm saying because it's just so distracting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that nobody else notices this crookedness in your face, not even when they're looking directly at you?\n\nPatient: I... I suppose it's technically possible that someone might not notice it. But it's really hard for me to believe that. \n\nWhen I look in the mirror or see those videos, it's just so glaringly obvious to me. It feels like it takes up my whole face. So, even if someone says they don't see it, I just assume they're being polite or... or they just don't want to make me feel worse. In my head, I just can't comprehend how they wouldn't see it when it's all I can see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your perception of your face being crooked and swollen is based on reality?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know. I mean, to me, it feels like reality. I look in the mirror or at those videos and it's right there. So I don't really know how to answer if it's based on reality, because... well, it's exactly what I see. I'm not sure what else to call it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face is actually normal and your concern about it being crooked and swollen is not based on how it really looks?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't think so. I hear what you're asking, but... no. I just can't wrap my head around the idea that it's actually normal. \n\nWhen I look at myself, the crookedness is just a fact to me. It's right there. So, I don't see how my concern could be made up or not based on how I actually look, because... well, to me, it's exactly how I look. I just don't see any other possibility.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else actually sees your face as crooked and swollen, or do you think they just humor you or don't want to hurt your feelings?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I really think they're just humoring me. Like I was saying a minute ago, it's just so obvious to me. \n\nI guess I can't know for an absolute fact what they actually see through their own eyes, but... if someone tells me I look fine, I just assume they're lying to protect my feelings. They have to see it. I just think they don't want to be cruel and point it out to my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked and swollen is a mistaken belief that is not based on reality?\n\nPatient: I... no, I don't think it's possible. I mean, I know you're asking if I could be mistaken, but... I just can't see how I could be. When I look in the mirror, it's just so clear to me. The swelling, the way it's crooked... it's just there. I don't think it's a mistake in my head. It's just what my face looks like.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, all the time. It's actually why I'm here. I teach high school, and lately we've had to record some of our lesson videos. When I watch them back, I can't stop seeing how crooked and swollen that side of my face looks. And then... even when I'm just standing in front of the classroom, not looking at a mirror or a screen, it's all I can think about. Once I notice it in my head, I feel like the students, the parents, the other staff... I feel like they must all be staring at it instead of listening to a single word I'm saying. So yeah, it's on my mind constantly, even when I can't actually see it.

It's become a really big problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face actually looks normal and you're just being overly critical of yourself?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. The crookedness is just... it's so obvious. I understand why you'd ask that, but to me, it's very real. I can't just unsee it.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: No, it's... it's really just my face. I don't worry about the rest of my body. I mean, the rest of me is fine. It's really just that one side of my face that looks so wrong. That's the only thing I focus on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for someone else to look at the same video and not see the crookedness you're talking about?\n\nPatient: I mean, maybe? I guess it's possible they might not notice it right away if they aren't looking closely. \n\nBut... like I mentioned earlier about those lesson videos, I just can't stop seeing how crooked and swollen it is. So even if someone else claims they don't see it, I just can't shake the feeling that the students, the parents, the other staff... they must all be staring right at it. I suppose it's technically possible that someone might not notice it. But it's really hard for me to believe that. \n\nWhen I look in the mirror or see those videos, it's just so glaringly obvious to me. It feels like it takes up my whole face. So, even if someone says they don't see it, I just assume they're being polite or... or they just don't want to make me feel worse. In my head, I just can't comprehend how they wouldn't see it when it's all I can see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your perception of your face being crooked and swollen is based on reality?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know. I mean, to me, it feels like reality. I look in the mirror or at those videos and it's right there. So I don't really know how to answer if it's based on reality, because... well, it's exactly what I see. I'm not sure what else to call it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face is actually normal and your concern about it being crooked and swollen is not based on how it really looks?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't think so. I hear what you're asking, but... no. I just can't wrap my head around the idea that it's actually normal. \n\nWhen I look at myself, the crookedness is just a fact to me. It's right there. So, I don't see how my concern could be made up or not based on how I actually look, because... well, to me, it's exactly how I look. I just don't see any other possibility.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else actually sees your face as crooked and swollen, or do you think they just humor you or don't want to hurt your feelings?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I really think they're just humoring me. I just think they don't want to be cruel and point it out to my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked and swollen is a mistaken belief that is not based on reality?\n\nPatient: I... no, I don't think it's possible. So, yes, I think it would still look swollen and off to me, no matter who told me otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked and swollen would ever change, no matter what anyone else told you or what evidence you were shown?\n\nPatient: I just... That's the only thing I focus on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for someone else to look at the same video and not see the crookedness you're talking about?\n\nPatient: I mean, maybe? I guess it's possible they might not notice it right away if they aren't looking closely. \n\nBut... like I mentioned earlier about those lesson videos, I just can't stop seeing how crooked and swollen it is. Once I notice it on the screen, it's literally all I can focus on. So even if someone else claims they don't see it, I just can't shake the feeling that the students, the parents, the other staff... they must all be staring right at it. I really feel like they aren't even listening to anything I'm saying because it's just so distracting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that nobody else notices this crookedness in your face, not even when they're looking directly at you?\n\nPatient: I... I suppose it's technically possible that someone might not notice it. But it's really hard for me to believe that. \n\nWhen I look in the mirror or see those videos, it's just so glaringly obvious to me. It feels like it takes up my whole face. So, even if someone says they don't see it, I just assume they're being polite or... or they just don't want to make me feel worse. In my head, I just can't comprehend how they wouldn't see it when it's all I can see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your perception of your face being crooked and swollen is based on reality?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know. I mean, to me, it feels like reality. I look in the mirror or at those videos and it's right there. So I don't really know how to answer if it's based on reality, because... well, it's exactly what I see. I'm not sure what else to call it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face is actually normal and your concern about it being crooked and swollen is not based on how it really looks?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't think so. I hear what you're asking, but... no. I just can't wrap my head around the idea that it's actually normal. \n\nWhen I look at myself, the crookedness is just a fact to me. It's right there. So, I don't see how my concern could be made up or not based on how I actually look, because... well, to me, it's exactly how I look. I just don't see any other possibility.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else actually sees your face as crooked and swollen, or do you think they just humor you or don't want to hurt your feelings?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I really think they're just humoring me. Like I was saying a minute ago, it's just so obvious to me. \n\nI guess I can't know for an absolute fact what they actually see through their own eyes, but... if someone tells me I look fine, I just assume they're lying to protect my feelings. They have to see it. I just think they don't want to be cruel and point it out to my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked and swollen is a mistaken belief that is not based on reality?\n\nPatient: I... no, I don't think it's possible. The swelling, the way it's crooked... it's just there. I don't think it's a mistake in my head. It's just what my face looks like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would still look crooked and swollen to you even if a plastic surgeon told you that there's nothing wrong with it?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I do. So, yes, I think it would still look swollen and off to me, no matter who told me otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked and swollen would ever change, no matter what anyone else told you or what evidence you were shown?\n\nPatient: I just... So... no, I really don't see my mind ever changing on this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the appearance of your face at times when you're not looking in a mirror or watching a video of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. I mean, even outside of just standing in front of the kids, it's affecting all my other responsibilities. I'm just too distracted by all of this. \n\nIt's actually gotten to the point where the administrators have started documenting my missed deadlines and some coverage problems I've had. In my head, I just can't comprehend how they wouldn't see it when it's all I can see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your perception of your face being crooked and swollen is based on reality?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know. I mean, to me, it feels like reality. I look in the mirror or at those videos and it's right there. So I don't really know how to answer if it's based on reality, because... well, it's exactly what I see. I'm not sure what else to call it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face is actually normal and your concern about it being crooked and swollen is not based on how it really looks?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't think so. I hear what you're asking, but... no. I just can't wrap my head around the idea that it's actually normal. \n\nWhen I look at myself, the crookedness is just a fact to me. It's right there. So, I don't see how my concern could be made up or not based on how I actually look, because... well, to me, it's exactly how I look. I just don't see any other possibility.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else actually sees your face as crooked and swollen, or do you think they just humor you or don't want to hurt your feelings?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I really think they're just humoring me. Like I was saying a minute ago, it's just so obvious to me. \n\nI guess I can't know for an absolute fact what they actually see through their own eyes, but... if someone tells me I look fine, I just assume they're lying to protect my feelings. They have to see it. I just think they don't want to be cruel and point it out to my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked and swollen is a mistaken belief that is not based on reality?\n\nPatient: I... no, I don't think it's possible. I mean, I know you're asking if I could be mistaken, but... I just can't see how I could be. When I look in the mirror, it's just so clear to me. The swelling, the way it's crooked... it's just there. I don't think it's a mistake in my head. It's just what my face looks like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would still look crooked and swollen to you even if a plastic surgeon told you that there's nothing wrong with it?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I do. Even if a surgeon told me that, I don't think it would change what I see. \n\nI've actually thought about that before. If an expert looked at me and said I was fine, I'd probably just think they weren't looking closely enough, or maybe they just didn't want to deal with me. Because... well, when I look in the mirror, the crookedness is just right there. It doesn't go away just because someone else says it isn't real. So, yes, I think it would still look swollen and off to me, no matter who told me otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked and swollen would ever change, no matter what anyone else told you or what evidence you were shown?\n\nPatient: I just... I don't think it would ever change. I don't even know what kind of evidence someone could show me, to be honest. \n\nIt's my face. No matter what people say or what they try to prove to me, I'm still going to wake up, look in the mirror, and see the exact same crookedness and swelling. It's just reality to me. So... no, I really don't see my mind ever changing on this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the appearance of your face at times when you're not looking in a mirror or watching a video of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. It's actually why I'm here. I teach high school, and lately we've had to record some of our lesson videos. When I watch them back, I can't stop seeing how crooked and swollen that side of my face looks. And then... even when I'm just standing in front of the classroom, not looking at a mirror or a screen, it's all I can think about. Once I notice it in my head, I feel like the students, the parents, the other staff... I feel like they must all be staring at it instead of listening to a single word I'm saying. So yeah, it's on my mind constantly, even when I can't actually see it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your concerns about your face are affecting other areas of your life outside of teaching?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. I mean, even outside of just standing in front of the kids, it's affecting all my other responsibilities. I've fallen way behind on my lesson planning, and... well, I also do some mentoring and coach the basketball team, but so much of my work just never gets finished on time anymore. I'm just too distracted by all of this. \n\nIt's actually gotten to the point where the administrators have started documenting my missed deadlines and some coverage problems I've had. Honestly, I'm really worried that I'm close to losing my position.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or events outside of work because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... outside of work, things are mostly okay socially. I still see my friends and my family, and I don't really avoid going out with them. I guess because I'm close to them, I don't feel as much like they're staring at it. It's really just at school where it's tearing everything apart. But as far as my social life goes, no, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I just think they don't want to be cruel and point it out to my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked and swollen is a mistaken belief that is not based on reality?\n\nPatient: I... no, I don't think it's possible. I mean, I know you're asking if I could be mistaken, but... I just can't see how I could be. When I look in the mirror, it's just so clear to me. The swelling, the way it's crooked... it's just there. I don't think it's a mistake in my head. It's just what my face looks like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would still look crooked and swollen to you even if a plastic surgeon told you that there's nothing wrong with it?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I do. Even if a surgeon told me that, I don't think it would change what I see. \n\nI've actually thought about that before. If an expert looked at me and said I was fine, I'd probably just think they weren't looking closely enough, or maybe they just didn't want to deal with me. Because... well, when I look in the mirror, the crookedness is just right there. It doesn't go away just because someone else says it isn't real. So, yes, I think it would still look swollen and off to me, no matter who told me otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked and swollen would ever change, no matter what anyone else told you or what evidence you were shown?\n\nPatient: I just... I don't think it would ever change. I don't even know what kind of evidence someone could show me, to be honest. \n\nIt's my face. No matter what people say or what they try to prove to me, I'm still going to wake up, look in the mirror, and see the exact same crookedness and swelling. It's just reality to me. So... no, I really don't see my mind ever changing on this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the appearance of your face at times when you're not looking in a mirror or watching a video of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. It's actually why I'm here. I teach high school, and lately we've had to record some of our lesson videos. When I watch them back, I can't stop seeing how crooked and swollen that side of my face looks. And then... even when I'm just standing in front of the classroom, not looking at a mirror or a screen, it's all I can think about. Once I notice it in my head, I feel like the students, the parents, the other staff... I feel like they must all be staring at it instead of listening to a single word I'm saying. I mean, even outside of just standing in front of the kids, it's affecting all my other responsibilities. I've fallen way behind on my lesson planning, and... well, I also do some mentoring and coach the basketball team, but so much of my work just never gets finished on time anymore. I'm just too distracted by all of this. \n\nIt's actually gotten to the point where the administrators have started documenting my missed deadlines and some coverage problems I've had. Honestly, I'm really worried that I'm close to losing my position.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or events outside of work because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... outside of work, things are mostly okay socially. I still see my friends and my family, and I don't really avoid going out with them. I guess because I'm close to them, I don't feel as much like they're staring at it. It's really just at school where it's tearing everything apart. But as far as my social life goes, no, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face would change if you received a second opinion from another psychiatrist?\n\nPatient: I don't know... If an expert looked at me and said I was fine, I'd probably just think they weren't looking closely enough, or maybe they just didn't want to deal with me. Because... well, when I look in the mirror, the crookedness is just right there. It doesn't go away just because someone else says it isn't real. So, yes, I think it would still look swollen and off to me, no matter who told me otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked and swollen would ever change, no matter what anyone else told you or what evidence you were shown?\n\nPatient: I just... I don't think it would ever change. I don't even know what kind of evidence someone could show me, to be honest. \n\nIt's my face. No matter what people say or what they try to prove to me, I'm still going to wake up, look in the mirror, and see the exact same crookedness and swelling. It's just reality to me. So... no, I really don't see my mind ever changing on this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the appearance of your face at times when you're not looking in a mirror or watching a video of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. When I watch them back, I can't stop seeing how crooked and swollen that side of my face looks. And then... even when I'm just standing in front of the classroom, not looking at a mirror or a screen, it's all I can think about. Once I notice it in my head, I feel like the students, the parents, the other staff... I feel like they must all be staring at it instead of listening to a single word I'm saying. So yeah, it's on my mind constantly, even when I can't actually see it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your concerns about your face are affecting other areas of your life outside of teaching?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. I mean, even outside of just standing in front of the kids, it's affecting all my other responsibilities. I've fallen way behind on my lesson planning, and... well, I also do some mentoring and coach the basketball team, but so much of my work just never gets finished on time anymore. I'm just too distracted by all of this. \n\nIt's actually gotten to the point where the administrators have started documenting my missed deadlines and some coverage problems I've had. Honestly, I'm really worried that I'm close to losing my position.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or events outside of work because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... outside of work, things are mostly okay socially. I still see my friends and my family, and I don't really avoid going out with them. I guess because I'm close to them, I don't feel as much like they're staring at it. It's really just at school where it's tearing everything apart. It's just what my face looks like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would still look crooked and swollen to you even if a plastic surgeon told you that there's nothing wrong with it?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I do. Even if a surgeon told me that, I don't think it would change what I see. \n\nI've actually thought about that before. If an expert looked at me and said I was fine, I'd probably just think they weren't looking closely enough, or maybe they just didn't want to deal with me. Because... well, when I look in the mirror, the crookedness is just right there. It doesn't go away just because someone else says it isn't real. So, yes, I think it would still look swollen and off to me, no matter who told me otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked and swollen would ever change, no matter what anyone else told you or what evidence you were shown?\n\nPatient: I just... I don't think it would ever change. I don't even know what kind of evidence someone could show me, to be honest. \n\nIt's my face. No matter what people say or what they try to prove to me, I'm still going to wake up, look in the mirror, and see the exact same crookedness and swelling. It's just reality to me. So... no, I really don't see my mind ever changing on this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the appearance of your face at times when you're not looking in a mirror or watching a video of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. It's actually why I'm here. I teach high school, and lately we've had to record some of our lesson videos. When I watch them back, I can't stop seeing how crooked and swollen that side of my face looks. And then... even when I'm just standing in front of the classroom, not looking at a mirror or a screen, it's all I can think about. Once I notice it in my head, I feel like the students, the parents, the other staff... I feel like they must all be staring at it instead of listening to a single word I'm saying. So yeah, it's on my mind constantly, even when I can't actually see it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your concerns about your face are affecting other areas of your life outside of teaching?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. I mean, even outside of just standing in front of the kids, it's affecting all my other responsibilities. I've fallen way behind on my lesson planning, and... well, I also do some mentoring and coach the basketball team, but so much of my work just never gets finished on time anymore. I'm just too distracted by all of this. \n\nIt's actually gotten to the point where the administrators have started documenting my missed deadlines and some coverage problems I've had. Honestly, I'm really worried that I'm close to losing my position.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or events outside of work because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... outside of work, things are mostly okay socially. I still see my friends and my family, and I don't really avoid going out with them. I guess because I'm close to them, I don't feel as much like they're staring at it. It's really just at school where it's tearing everything apart. But as far as my social life goes, no, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face would change if you received a second opinion from another psychiatrist?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I mean, I guess I'd listen to what another doctor has to say, but I don't really see how it would change anything. I know what I see when I look at the videos, and I know how it feels when I'm standing in front of the classroom. Hearing someone else tell me it's not real or that I shouldn't worry about it doesn't make the swelling or the crookedness go away. So, honestly, no, I don't think a second opinion would really change what I believe about my face.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations outside of teaching where you feel your concern about your face is causing you significant distress or interfering with your ability to function?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Like I said, it's mostly just at school where it's a huge problem. When I'm at home or just out running errands, I mean, I still think about it, and it bothers me... but it doesn't stop me from doing what I need to do. It's really just the teaching and being in front of all those people that makes it unbearable. Outside of that, I can mostly function fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look crooked and swollen to you even if multiple people, including experts, told you it looks normal and showed you pictures and videos as proof?\n\nPatient: Honestly... yeah, I think it still would. I can physically see the swelling and the crookedness with my own eyes. So... just having people tell me it looks normal isn't going to magically change what I'm actually seeing. I'm pretty sure I'd still see it exactly the same way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I really don't think so. I hear what you're asking, but... no. I just can't wrap my head around the idea that it's actually normal. \n\nWhen I look at myself, the crookedness is just a fact to me. It's right there. So, I don't see how my concern could be made up or not based on how I actually look, because... well, to me, it's exactly how I look. I just don't see any other possibility.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else actually sees your face as crooked and swollen, or do you think they just humor you or don't want to hurt your feelings?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I really think they're just humoring me. Like I was saying a minute ago, it's just so obvious to me. \n\nI guess I can't know for an absolute fact what they actually see through their own eyes, but... if someone tells me I look fine, I just assume they're lying to protect my feelings. They have to see it. I just think they don't want to be cruel and point it out to my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked and swollen is a mistaken belief that is not based on reality?\n\nPatient: I... no, I don't think it's possible. I mean, I know you're asking if I could be mistaken, but... I just can't see how I could be. When I look in the mirror, it's just so clear to me. The swelling, the way it's crooked... it's just there. I don't think it's a mistake in my head. It's just what my face looks like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would still look crooked and swollen to you even if a plastic surgeon told you that there's nothing wrong with it?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I do. Even if a surgeon told me that, I don't think it would change what I see. \n\nI've actually thought about that before. If an expert looked at me and said I was fine, I'd probably just think they weren't looking closely enough, or maybe they just didn't want to deal with me. Because... well, when I look in the mirror, the crookedness is just right there. It doesn't go away just because someone else says it isn't real. So, yes, I think it would still look swollen and off to me, no matter who told me otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked and swollen would ever change, no matter what anyone else told you or what evidence you were shown?\n\nPatient: I just... I don't think it would ever change. I don't even know what kind of evidence someone could show me, to be honest. \n\nIt's my face. No matter what people say or what they try to prove to me, I'm still going to wake up, look in the mirror, and see the exact same crookedness and swelling. It's just reality to me. So... no, I really don't see my mind ever changing on this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the appearance of your face at times when you're not looking in a mirror or watching a video of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. It's actually why I'm here. I teach high school, and lately we've had to record some of our lesson videos. When I watch them back, I can't stop seeing how crooked and swollen that side of my face looks. And then... even when I'm just standing in front of the classroom, not looking at a mirror or a screen, it's all I can think about. Once I notice it in my head, I feel like the students, the parents, the other staff... I feel like they must all be staring at it instead of listening to a single word I'm saying. So yeah, it's on my mind constantly, even when I can't actually see it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your concerns about your face are affecting other areas of your life outside of teaching?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. I mean, even outside of just standing in front of the kids, it's affecting all my other responsibilities. I've fallen way behind on my lesson planning, and... well, I also do some mentoring and coach the basketball team, but so much of my work just never gets finished on time anymore. I'm just too distracted by all of this. \n\nIt's actually gotten to the point where the administrators have started documenting my missed deadlines and some coverage problems I've had. Honestly, I'm really worried that I'm close to losing my position.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or events outside of work because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... outside of work, things are mostly okay socially. I still see my friends and my family, and I don't really avoid going out with them. I guess because I'm close to them, I don't feel as much like they're staring at it. It's really just at school where it's tearing everything apart. But as far as my social life goes, no, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face would change if you received a second opinion from another psychiatrist?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I mean, I guess I'd listen to what another doctor has to say, but I don't really see how it would change anything. I know what I see when I look at the videos, and I know how it feels when I'm standing in front of the classroom. Hearing someone else tell me it's not real or that I shouldn't worry about it doesn't make the swelling or the crookedness go away. So, honestly, no, I don't think a second opinion would really change what I believe about my face.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations outside of teaching where you feel your concern about your face is causing you significant distress or interfering with your ability to function?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Like I said, it's mostly just at school where it's a huge problem. When I'm at home or just out running errands, I mean, I still think about it, and it bothers me... but it doesn't stop me from doing what I need to do. It's really just the teaching and being in front of all those people that makes it unbearable. Outside of that, I can mostly function fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look crooked and swollen to you even if multiple people, including experts, told you it looks normal and showed you pictures and videos as proof?\n\nPatient: Honestly... yeah, I think it still would. I mean, I see what I see. Even if a bunch of experts stood there and showed me pictures or videos, I'm the one looking at them, you know? I can physically see the swelling and the crookedness with my own eyes. So... just having people tell me it looks normal isn't going to magically change what I'm actually seeing. I'm pretty sure I'd still see it exactly the same way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations outside of teaching where you feel like your concern about your face is making it hard for you to enjoy time with friends or family?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I mentioned before, it's really just the teaching part that gets to me. When I'm with family or hanging out with friends, I still know my face looks the way it does, but it doesn't stop me from enjoying my time with them. I guess because I'm comfortable around them and I know they aren't judging me the way a classroom full of high schoolers would. So, no, it doesn't really get in the way of my personal life like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I just feel like the students, the parents, the rest of the staff... they must all be staring at it too, instead of actually listening to anything I'm teaching. It's become a really big problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face actually looks normal and you're just being overly critical of yourself?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. I mean, people have told me that before, that I'm just being hard on myself. But I just don't see how that's possible. \n\nWhen I look at the videos, it's right there. The crookedness is just... it's so obvious. I understand why you'd ask that, but to me, it's very real. I can't just unsee it.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: No, it's... it's really just my face. I don't worry about the rest of my body. I mean, the rest of me is fine. It's really just that one side of my face that looks so wrong. That's the only thing I focus on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for someone else to look at the same video and not see the crookedness you're talking about?\n\nPatient: I mean, maybe? I guess it's possible they might not notice it right away if they aren't looking closely. \n\nBut... like I mentioned earlier about those lesson videos, I just can't stop seeing how crooked and swollen it is. Once I notice it on the screen, it's literally all I can focus on. So even if someone else claims they don't see it, I just can't shake the feeling that the students, the parents, the other staff... they must all be staring right at it. I really feel like they aren't even listening to anything I'm saying because it's just so distracting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that nobody else notices this crookedness in your face, not even when they're looking directly at you?\n\nPatient: I... I suppose it's technically possible that someone might not notice it. But it's really hard for me to believe that. \n\nWhen I look in the mirror or see those videos, it's just so glaringly obvious to me. It feels like it takes up my whole face. So, even if someone says they don't see it, I just assume they're being polite or... or they just don't want to make me feel worse. In my head, I just can't comprehend how they wouldn't see it when it's all I can see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your perception of your face being crooked and swollen is based on reality?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know. I mean, to me, it feels like reality. I look in the mirror or at those videos and it's right there. So I don't really know how to answer if it's based on reality, because... well, it's exactly what I see. I'm not sure what else to call it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face is actually normal and your concern about it being crooked and swollen is not based on how it really looks?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't think so. I hear what you're asking, but... no. I just can't wrap my head around the idea that it's actually normal. \n\nWhen I look at myself, the crookedness is just a fact to me. It's right there. So, I don't see how my concern could be made up or not based on how I actually look, because... well, to me, it's exactly how I look. I just don't see any other possibility.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else actually sees your face as crooked and swollen, or do you think they just humor you or don't want to hurt your feelings?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I really think they're just humoring me. Like I was saying a minute ago, it's just so obvious to me. \n\nI guess I can't know for an absolute fact what they actually see through their own eyes, but... if someone tells me I look fine, I just assume they're lying to protect my feelings. They have to see it. I just think they don't want to be cruel and point it out to my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked and swollen is a mistaken belief that is not based on reality?\n\nPatient: I... no, I don't think it's possible. I mean, I know you're asking if I could be mistaken, but... I just can't see how I could be. When I look in the mirror, it's just so clear to me. The swelling, the way it's crooked... it's just there. I don't think it's a mistake in my head. It's just what my face looks like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would still look crooked and swollen to you even if a plastic surgeon told you that there's nothing wrong with it?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I do. Even if a surgeon told me that, I don't think it would change what I see. \n\nI've actually thought about that before. If an expert looked at me and said I was fine, I'd probably just think they weren't looking closely enough, or maybe they just didn't want to deal with me. Because... well, when I look in the mirror, the crookedness is just right there. It doesn't go away just because someone else says it isn't real. So, yes, I think it would still look swollen and off to me, no matter who told me otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked and swollen would ever change, no matter what anyone else told you or what evidence you were shown?\n\nPatient: I just... I don't think it would ever change. I don't even know what kind of evidence someone could show me, to be honest. \n\nIt's my face. No matter what people say or what they try to prove to me, I'm still going to wake up, look in the mirror, and see the exact same crookedness and swelling. It's just reality to me. So... no, I really don't see my mind ever changing on this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the appearance of your face at times when you're not looking in a mirror or watching a video of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. It's actually why I'm here. I teach high school, and lately we've had to record some of our lesson videos. When I watch them back, I can't stop seeing how crooked and swollen that side of my face looks. And then... even when I'm just standing in front of the classroom, not looking at a mirror or a screen, it's all I can think about. Once I notice it in my head, I feel like the students, the parents, the other staff... I feel like they must all be staring at it instead of listening to a single word I'm saying. So yeah, it's on my mind constantly, even when I can't actually see it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your concerns about your face are affecting other areas of your life outside of teaching?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. I mean, even outside of just standing in front of the kids, it's affecting all my other responsibilities. I've fallen way behind on my lesson planning, and... well, I also do some mentoring and coach the basketball team, but so much of my work just never gets finished on time anymore. I'm just too distracted by all of this. \n\nIt's actually gotten to the point where the administrators have started documenting my missed deadlines and some coverage problems I've had. Honestly, I'm really worried that I'm close to losing my position.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or events outside of work because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... outside of work, things are mostly okay socially. I still see my friends and my family, and I don't really avoid going out with them. I guess because I'm close to them, I don't feel as much like they're staring at it. It's really just at school where it's tearing everything apart. But as far as my social life goes, no, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face would change if you received a second opinion from another psychiatrist?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I mean, I guess I'd listen to what another doctor has to say, but I don't really see how it would change anything. I know what I see when I look at the videos, and I know how it feels when I'm standing in front of the classroom. Hearing someone else tell me it's not real or that I shouldn't worry about it doesn't make the swelling or the crookedness go away. So, honestly, no, I don't think a second opinion would really change what I believe about my face.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations outside of teaching where you feel your concern about your face is causing you significant distress or interfering with your ability to function?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Like I said, it's mostly just at school where it's a huge problem. When I'm at home or just out running errands, I mean, I still think about it, and it bothers me... but it doesn't stop me from doing what I need to do. It's really just the teaching and being in front of all those people that makes it unbearable. Outside of that, I can mostly function fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look crooked and swollen to you even if multiple people, including experts, told you it looks normal and showed you pictures and videos as proof?\n\nPatient: Honestly... yeah, I think it still would. I mean, I see what I see. Even if a bunch of experts stood there and showed me pictures or videos, I'm the one looking at them, you know? I can physically see the swelling and the crookedness with my own eyes. So... just having people tell me it looks normal isn't going to magically change what I'm actually seeing. I'm pretty sure I'd still see it exactly the same way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations outside of teaching where you feel like your concern about your face is making it hard for you to enjoy time with friends or family?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I mentioned before, it's really just the teaching part that gets to me. When I'm with family or hanging out with friends, I still know my face looks the way it does, but it doesn't stop me from enjoying my time with them. I guess because I'm comfortable around them and I know they aren't judging me the way a classroom full of high schoolers would. So, no, it doesn't really get in the way of my personal life like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face looks crooked and swollen even when you're not thinking about what others might see?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean, whenever I look in the mirror, I just see it. The swelling and how crooked it is, it's just right there. I don't really know if I'm actively thinking about other people in that exact second... I just know what I'm looking at. So, I guess I haven't really noticed if it changes based on whether I'm thinking about other people or not.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
